Since Phase 2 was only ever a one-line bullet list in ARCHITECTURE.md §16 (not a scoped plan like Phase 1 had), asked the user to choose a direction before writing any code: a second industry blueprint, a new module on the IT blueprint, or platform hardening (audit logging/email). **User chose: a new module.** Follow-up choice among HR/CRM/Meetings/Calendar/Documents — **user chose HR**, specifically drawn to closing the real "Member with no department sees nothing" gap flagged back in Stage 9 (CONTEXT.md §17).

#### Session 2 continued — Phase 2, Stage 1: HR module (Departments, Teams, Employee org placement) (complete)
- **Entered plan mode, designed, and got approval before writing code** — same rigor as Phase 1's stages, appended as a new section to the plan file (not folded into the completed Phase 1 plan or the separate Team-invite addendum).
- **The headline result: this module makes a year-old piece of dead schema actually work.** `Department`/`Team` tables (self-referencing hierarchy) and `User.departmentId`/`teamId` have existed since **Stage 1** — nothing had ever created a Department/Team row or set a User's placement, so every `team`/`department`-scoped permission check (`project:read:team`, `task:read:team`, granted to `role.member` since the very first blueprint fixture) was structurally unreachable. This module doesn't just add HR surface area, it's the first thing that ever exercises that RBAC path for real.
- **`User` gained three nullable columns** (`jobTitle`, `employmentStatus` — free-text UI vocabulary like `Task.status`, not a DB enum — and `startDate`; migration `20260715101154_hr_module_user_fields`), extending the existing row rather than introducing a parallel `Employee` table, since every `User` in this app already *is* an employee 1:1 by construction (same reasoning as why `departmentId`/`teamId` already live directly on `User`).
- **One new permission triple**: `department:manage:tenant`, added to `role.admin`. Reused the existing `user:manage:tenant` grant (not a new triple) for `user.assignDepartment`, since that mutation changes a `User` row's own fields — consistent with where `user.invite` already lives.
- **New module `apps/api/src/modules/hr/`** (`departments.list`, `teams.list`, `department.create`, `team.create`, same registrar pattern as every prior module) **+ `user.assignDepartment` added to `users.mutations.ts`** (lives with the other `user.*` mutations, not in the HR module, since it mutates `User`) **+ `users.list`'s shape extended** (department/team names resolved, `jobTitle`, `employmentStatus`) rather than standing up a duplicate roster source.
- **New composite `OrgStructure`** (`apps/web/src/modules/hr/OrgStructure.tsx`): department list with inline hierarchical create (optional parent), team list scoped to a department, and a roster with a per-person department/team assignment control — all three sections independently presence-gated on their respective actions surviving pruning, same pattern as every prior composite. New `nav.hr` → `page.hr`, Admin-only (`requiredPermission: "department:manage"`), same treatment as `nav.team`.
- **Two real Prisma 7 process gotchas hit and fixed, worth remembering:** (1) `prisma migrate dev` in this project's `prisma.config.ts` setup did **not** automatically regenerate the Prisma Client afterward — a plain `pnpm exec prisma generate` was needed before the new `User` fields typechecked, even though the migration itself applied cleanly. (2) This is the same *family* of gotcha as Stage 11's finding that `migrate reset` doesn't auto-seed — **any Prisma command in this project that's supposed to have a "then also do X" follow-on step should be verified, not assumed**, since this Prisma 7 + custom `prisma.config.ts` combination doesn't reliably chain those steps the way vanilla Prisma docs describe.
- **Verified live against the real Supabase project, in two parts, again without ever using the real Admin's password:** (A) a full run under a disposable throwaway tenant with a genuinely separate throwaway Member — created a Department and a Team under it, confirmed a department-scoped project was **invisible** to the Member beforehand (proving the gap really existed, not just asserting it), assigned the Member to that Department/Team via `user.assignDepartment`, then confirmed the exact same `projects.list` call now **returned the project** — the gap-closing behavior demonstrated end-to-end, not just unit-tested in isolation; also confirmed `users.list` correctly reflects the new `departmentName`/`teamName`. (B) a negative check against the real "Magar Limited" tenant's standing Member account: `nav.hr` absent, `department.create` → `403`.
- No new unit tests this stage — `department.create`/`team.create`/`user.assignDepartment` are thin CRUD with no branching logic worth extracting as a pure function (same as `project.create`/`project.update`, which also have no spec file). 66 backend + 1 frontend tests still pass, typecheck clean, both confirmed *after* the explicit `prisma generate` fix above.
- Rebuilt the API, reseeded the blueprint (bumped `Blueprint.updatedAt`, so the user's existing browser session picks up `nav.hr` automatically), restarted; confirmed `HrModule dependencies initialized` in a clean boot log.

#### User confirmed HR module works, requested a strategic pivot: Stage 12 (Prototype Polish)
- User tested departments/teams/assignment as Admin, confirmed everything works, then asked to pause new-module work and instead turn the existing app into a polished, presentable prototype — "enterprise-grade AI-powered workspace platform," styled after Linear/Vercel/Notion/GitHub/Stripe Dashboard, while strictly preserving the Config Engine/SDUI/RBAC/tenant-isolation architecture. Asked "how would you approach this" — responded with a recommendation (rendering-layer pass, not new modules) rather than starting immediately, then clarified three real forks via `AskUserQuestion` (lucide-react vs. hand-rolled icons → **lucide-react**; wire FilterBar for real vs. remove it → **wire it up**; propose a visual direction vs. user-specified → **user gave a detailed brief**: dark-primary/light-secondary theme, Geist/Inter typography, neutral palette with indigo/violet/cyan accents, sparing glassmorphism, charts, a functional config-driven FilterBar, command-style interactions, desktop-first responsive). Entered plan mode, researched the actual current frontend state (confirmed **zero CSS infrastructure existed anywhere** — no globals.css, no Tailwind, no dark mode, all 17 primitives + 5 composites on scattered inline styles), and wrote a full addendum plan before any code — approved.

#### Session 2 continued — Stage 12: Prototype Polish (complete)
- **New frontend dependencies**: `lucide-react` (icons), `tailwindcss` v4 + `@tailwindcss/postcss` (CSS-first `@theme` config, no JS config file), `geist` (Vercel's official font package via `next/font`), `next-themes` (flash-free dark/light toggle, dark as default), `recharts` (the one dashboard chart).
- **Design tokens** (`apps/web/src/app/globals.css`): a full light/dark palette as plain CSS custom properties (`--bg`, `--surface`, `--border`, `--text`, `--accent`, `--danger`/`--success`/`--warning`/`--info`, etc.), mapped into Tailwind's utility namespace via `@theme { --color-x: var(--x) }` — meaning `bg-accent`/`text-text-muted`/etc. are real generated utility classes, but their *values* stay runtime-swappable. `next-themes` toggles a `.dark` class on `<html>`; **`Tenant.branding.accentColor`, if set, overrides `--accent` inline** on the workspace shell's root div — the first time `branding` (an inert empty JSON blob since Stage 1) actually does anything, proving "config-driven, per-tenant" visually.
- **New shared UI kit** (`apps/web/src/ui/`): `Button`, `Input`, `Select`, `Card`/`CardHeader`/`CardBody`, `Badge`, `Skeleton`/`SkeletonRows`, `Alert` (inline, form-level errors), `Toast`/`useToast()` (global, for action-success feedback — a `ToastProvider` mounted once in the root layout), `Dropdown`/`DropdownItem` (outside-click-closing menu, powers the user menu and `ActionMenu` primitive), `CommandPalette` (`Cmd/Ctrl+K`, lists `manifest.navigation` — already permission-pruned/compiled, not a hardcoded route list — the one deliberate glassmorphism spot, on the overlay backdrop only), and `icons.ts` (a `NavItem.icon` string key → real lucide component lookup map, the first time that field, unused since Stage 8, actually renders anything).
- **The one genuine architecture addition, not just visual: page-scoped dynamic filter state.** `FilterBar` went from a static, disabled placeholder to a real controlled component whose *available filters and their options are still entirely blueprint-driven* (`props.filters: [{ key, label, type, options }]`, no hardcoding). New `apps/web/src/sdui/filter-state.tsx` (`FilterStateProvider`/`useFilterState()`, page-scoped, keyed by page id so navigating away always clears it) adds `filters` as a **third `{ ref: ... }` namespace** in `use-data-binding.ts`, alongside the existing `user`/`tenant` ones — so `page.projects`'s `projects-table` binds `params.status` to `{ ref: "filters.status" }`, and selecting a value in the FilterBar genuinely re-fetches the table. Verified this isn't just plumbing that compiles: scripted calls to `projects.list` with different `status` values return genuinely different result sets.
- **Workspace shell rebuilt**: a real header/topbar (page chrome, relocated `NotificationBell`, a user-menu dropdown with theme toggle + logout), sidebar with real icons and active-route highlighting that collapses to a slide-in drawer below `md`, all wrapped in the command palette.
- **All 17 primitives restyled** onto the token system + UI kit (in place — same props/schema contracts, zero behavior change), plus **one new primitive, `Chart`** (`recharts`, a single donut chart — deliberately not a general charting system with a dozen options, matching "the one chart requested," extendable later if a page needs more). Its colors are CSS `var()` strings (e.g. `var(--color-accent)`), not fixed hex — the chart is theme-reactive for free rather than needing a separate hardcoded palette. Backed by a new data source, `projects.statusBreakdown` (`apps/api/src/modules/projects/projects.data-sources.ts`, same `projectsWhere` scope-filtering as `projects.list`/`.count`, grouped via Prisma's `groupBy`), wired onto `page.dashboard`.
- **All 5 composites restyled** (`ProjectBoard`, `TaskList`, `TeamMembers`, `OrgStructure`, `NotificationBell`) — same behavior, now also firing a success `Toast` on create/invite/assign actions where they previously just silently refetched.
- **Auth pages rebuilt** (`/`, `/login`, `/signup`) on the new kit with a subtle branded radial-gradient background — the first thing a prospective user/investor/recruiter sees now looks intentional rather than a bare HTML form.
- **A real, disclosed limitation: I cannot take screenshots or drive a browser myself.** Verification for this stage was therefore: (1) `pnpm -r run typecheck` and `pnpm -r run test` clean throughout (66 backend + 1 frontend, **unchanged** — confirming the restyle didn't alter behavior the existing tests assert); (2) direct inspection of server-rendered HTML output (confirmed Tailwind classes, font variables, and the theme-init script are all present and correct); (3) a full scripted pass against the live Supabase project confirming zero regressions (bootstrap manifest, all 5 nav items, Team invite, HR department creation, Member-role pruning) *and* the two new capabilities (Chart's data source, FilterBar's underlying status-filtering mechanism) — but the actual interactive, visual result has **not** been seen by me and needs the user's real browser review, same as always, just more load-bearing this time since "does it look good" is inherently not something a script can answer.
- Reseeded the blueprint (new `Chart` node, `FilterBar` config, `Table` binding) — bumped `Blueprint.updatedAt` automatically (the Stage-9 etag fix), so the user's existing session picks up every change with a normal reload, no cache clearing needed. Rebuilt and restarted the API; cleared `apps/web/.next` and restarted the dev server fresh given the scale of frontend changes (new Tailwind/PostCSS config, per the established build/dev-cache lesson).

#### User reviewed Stage 12 live, confirmed the direction, and laid out a large Phase 2 wishlist
- User tested Stage 12 in the browser: "the overall UI is heading in the right direction... much more modern and polished." Confirmed the architecture (Config Engine/SDUI/RBAC/multi-tenancy) must remain the foundation of the *production* product, not just the prototype — every future module goes through the same path, never a standalone implementation. Then listed twelve desired feature areas at once (Meetings, Team Chat, Calendar, Documents, HR expansion, CRM, Analytics, AI Assistant, Announcements, Email/Push, Settings, Integrations).
- Rather than plan all twelve together, proposed clustering them by what they actually require: modules that fit the existing registry pattern directly (CRM, Announcements, HR expansion minus payroll), modules needing one new capability first (Documents/file storage, Calendar, Settings, Analytics), and genuinely separate initiatives needing their own dedicated planning — Team Chat (real-time infra, explicitly deferred since Phase 1), AI Assistant (LLM/embeddings, explicitly out of scope until now), Integrations (external OAuth credentials only the user can provision), Email/Push (a real email/push provider, another explicit Phase 1 scope cut). User agreed to sequence one module at a time and, given a choice among CRM/Announcements/Settings, **chose Settings & Workspace Customization** — the most architecturally interesting of the three, since it's the first real UI over the Configuration Engine's Layer-2 tenant-config override system (alive since Stage 4, never reachable outside a seed script until now).

#### Session 2 continued — Phase 2, Stage 2: Settings & Workspace Customization (complete)
- **Deliberately bounded scope**: tenant branding (`accentColor`, the one field Stage 12 already made the frontend consume) and navigation-item **label** overrides via the exact `overrides.navigation.patch` mechanism `override-applier.ts` has supported since Stage 4. Explicitly not in scope: page-content overrides, adding/removing nav items, feature flags, or plan/entitlement management.
- **The core invariant this stage had to respect, not reintroduce a bug around:** CONTEXT.md already flagged that no code path had ever mutated an existing `TenantConfig` row in place, and that doing so would need the same "insert a new version, never edit in place" discipline as `Blueprint.updatedAt`. `workspaceConfig.updateNavigationLabel` (`apps/api/src/modules/settings/settings.mutations.ts`) honors this: it deactivates the current active `TenantConfig` row and inserts a new one (`version + 1`) in the same transaction, merging the new label into `overrides.navigation.patch` via a pure, exported, unit-tested function (`mergeNavigationLabelPatch` — preserves unrelated items' patches and any `remove`/`add` entries untouched, same "extract branching logic" convention as `projectsWhere`).
- **`tenant.updateBranding` needed a real injected service** (`TenantPrismaService.root` — `Tenant.branding` lives on a platform-root table with no `tenant_id`, not reachable via the tenant-scoped `tx`), so it uses the same factory-function pattern established for `user.invite`'s `SupabaseAdminService` need. Input strictly validated as a 6-digit hex color (`/^#[0-9a-f]{6}$/i`) — never trust a value that gets interpolated directly into a live CSS custom property. One new permission triple, `settings:manage:tenant`, Admin-only, gating both mutations and the new `nav.settings`/`page.settings`.
- **One real new frontend capability: refetching the whole workspace shell, not just one node's bound data.** Every prior composite only ever needed `useDataBinding().refetch()` — its own node's data. Settings changes (a nav label, the accent color) live in the *shell* (`manifest.navigation`, `manifest.tenant.branding`), not any page's content, so `RenderContextValue` gained `refetchBootstrap: () => void` (`apps/web/src/sdui/render-context.tsx`), backed by `WorkspaceLayout`'s bootstrap-fetch effect becoming a named, re-callable function. After a Settings save, the sidebar label / accent color update live, everywhere, with no manual reload.
- **New composite `WorkspaceSettings`** (`apps/web/src/modules/settings/WorkspaceSettings.tsx`): reads current branding/nav state from `useBootstrap()` (the full manifest, no new read data source needed — the compiled nav labels already reflect any prior override), writes via `callMutation` + `refetchBootstrap()`. Both sections independently presence-gated on their mutation surviving pruning.
- **A real bug found live and fixed, same class as the Stage 9 etag bug — this is exactly why "make it functional, then verify live" keeps paying off:** the etag never accounted for `Tenant.branding` changes at all (only `blueprintVersion`/`Blueprint.updatedAt`/`TenantConfig.version`/`permissionsHash`), because nothing had ever mutated `Tenant.branding` post-creation before this stage. Caught by scripting the exact scenario: change the accent color, re-send the *old* etag, confirm the server still incorrectly returned `304`. **Fixed** in `apps/api/src/config-engine/compiler.service.ts::resolveIdentity()` by folding `tenant.updatedAt` (already `@updatedAt`-managed, and the `tenant` row was already being fetched for `blueprintVersion` — free, no extra query) into the etag hash. Re-verified: old etag now correctly returns `200`, not `304`, after a branding change.
- **4 new unit tests** (`settings.mutations.spec.ts`, covering `mergeNavigationLabelPatch`'s merge/preserve behavior). 70 backend + 1 frontend, all passing.
- **Verified live against the real Supabase project, entirely through a throwaway tenant (never the real Admin's password)**: Member correctly lacks `nav.settings` and gets `403` on both mutations directly; branding change round-trips and produces a fresh etag; renaming `nav.projects` inserted a genuinely new `TenantConfig` v2 row (confirmed directly against the DB — old row `isActive: false`, new row `isActive: true`) and the compiled manifest immediately reflected it; renaming a *second* item (`nav.tasks`) produced a v3 row with **both** overrides intact — proving the merge doesn't clobber, not just asserting it.
- Rebuilt and restarted the API (confirmed `SettingsModule dependencies initialized`), reseeded the blueprint, restarted the web dev server cleanly (this stage touched `RenderContextValue`'s shape, a core context type, and an existing test fixture — `renderer.test.tsx` — needed a one-line update to supply the new `refetchBootstrap` field; a mechanical consequence of extending the interface, not a behavior regression).

#### Post-Settings bug — existing tenants never receive permissions added to a blueprint role after they've already signed up
- **User report:** logged in as the real Admin, no "Settings" option anywhere in the sidebar. Asked me to check whether `nav.settings` was actually in the compiled manifest or being incorrectly pruned.
- **Root cause, found by checking the real data, not guessed:** `Role.permissions` is a snapshot copied from the blueprint at tenant-provisioning time (`AuthService.signup`) and never re-derived live — `PermissionResolverService.resolveEffectivePermissions` reads that stored JSON directly (`rbac/permission-resolver.service.ts`), by deliberate design (a plain join, not a per-request JSON walk against the blueprint). Checked the real Admin's actual stored `Role.permissions`: it ended at `user:manage:tenant` — both `department:manage:tenant` (added during the HR module stage) and `settings:manage:tenant` (added this stage) were missing, because the tenant's role was materialized *before* either permission existed in the blueprint, and nothing ever pushed the update to already-provisioned tenants. **This means the HR module's "Org Structure" nav item had almost certainly been invisible to the real Admin too, the whole time since that stage** — my own HR verification only positively tested a throwaway tenant's Admin and negatively tested the real tenant's *Member*, never the real tenant's own Admin, so this was never caught until now.
- **Fixed** at the structural level, not just for this one tenant: `prisma/seed.ts`'s `main()` now re-syncs every existing tenant's blueprint-sourced `Role` rows (matched via `sourceBlueprintRoleId`, scoped to the blueprint's industry) to the blueprint's *current* permission list, every time the seed script runs. Safe to fully overwrite (not merge) because Phase 1 has no per-tenant role-customization mechanism (unlike nav/branding, which do have one via `TenantConfigOverrides`) — a blueprint-sourced role's permissions are supposed to always match the blueprint exactly. Ran it immediately: 6 existing roles across 3 tenants re-synced, confirmed the real Admin's `Role.permissions` now includes both missing grants.
- **Also found and cleaned up while investigating:** an orphaned throwaway tenant ("Settings Co ...") left behind by the *first* Settings verification run, which had thrown its assertion failure (the etag bug, since fixed) before reaching its own cleanup step — a real gap in that script's exception safety, not a systemic issue. Also confirmed the two tenants both named "Magar Limited" are **not** a bug: one is the real Admin's account, the other belongs to a second real signup (`deepsmag15@gmail.com`) created a few minutes apart post-Stage-11-reset — genuinely separate real data, left untouched.
- **The general lesson, worth remembering for every future module:** adding a new permission to a blueprint role only affects *new* signups automatically. Any existing tenant needs the seed script's re-sync step to actually receive it — and any live-verification methodology that only tests a *throwaway* tenant's Admin (to avoid touching the real Admin's password, the standing practice all session) will systematically miss this exact bug, because a throwaway tenant is always freshly provisioned with the latest blueprint. **Future verification should also confirm, via a DB-level check like this one (not a login), that the real tenant's materialized role permissions actually include any newly-added grant** — not just that a throwaway tenant's do.
- No new code paths beyond the seed script — full `pnpm -r run typecheck`/`test` still pass (70 backend + 1 frontend, unchanged). No server restart needed (only DB content changed, read fresh on every request).

#### User thanked, then raised five architectural suggestions before continuing
- User confirmed the Settings fix worked, then raised five points before more modules: (1) a unique Company/Workspace ID (real gap — `Tenant.name` has zero uniqueness, exactly what caused the two "Magar Limited" tenants), (2) requiring it at login, (3) Settings visible to everyone with edits still permission-gated, (4) a genuine Member-experience audit, (5) designing a real role hierarchy before building further modules. Investigated before proposing anything: `Role.extendsRoleId` exists in the schema but is **completely dead** — never read or written anywhere, despite the original architecture naming "extends-chain union" as the intended permission model. Also confirmed email is already globally unique in Supabase Auth and the JWT-minting hook already derives `tenant_id` from a strict 1:1 `auth_user_id → users` lookup — so (2) could not be about supporting one email across multiple tenants (structurally impossible today) unless that's a deliberate future direction.
- Clarified via `AskUserQuestion` (4 questions) before planning: user wants the Workspace ID as a **real, server-verified login field** (not cosmetic) while explicitly keeping "one email = one company"; **extends-chain inheritance** for the eventual role hierarchy (over flat tiers or department-scoped roles alone); **view-only** Settings for Members (no self-profile-editing yet); and the **Member-experience audit before** the role hierarchy, so it isn't run against a model that's about to change. Role hierarchy itself stays out of scope for this stage — its own dedicated plan later, given it touches every permission check in the app.

#### Session 2 continued — Identity & Member-Experience Foundation (complete)
- **`Tenant.workspaceId`** (new, `@unique`, nullable at the DB level — app-enforced invariant, not DB-enforced, avoiding the "NOT NULL on a non-empty table" migration friction already solved once for `Blueprint.updatedAt`). Auto-generated at signup from the company name (`slugify()`, pure + unit-tested, in new `apps/api/src/auth/workspace-id.ts`), with a numeric-suffix collision loop (`generateUniqueWorkspaceId`) — verified live: signing up two tenants with the *identical* company name produces two genuinely distinct slugs. Both existing real tenants backfilled (`magar-limited` and `magar-limited-2` for the two real "Magar Limited" accounts found earlier).
- **New `POST /auth/verify-workspace`** (public, unauthenticated, same precedent as `/auth/signup`): confirms an email genuinely belongs to the tenant identified by a given Workspace ID, called by the login page *before* it ever attempts a Supabase sign-in. Deliberately returns one generic error for every failure mode (wrong workspace, wrong email, nonexistent workspace) — verified live that a fake workspace ID and a wrong-email-for-a-real-workspace produce byte-identical error messages, closing an enumeration channel. **Does not change how `tenant_id` ends up in the JWT** — still derived purely from `auth_user_id`, unchanged; this is a pre-flight confirmation layer, not a new identity model, matching the user's explicit "keep one email = one company" direction.
- **A real bug caught by live verification, not inspection:** the first version of `verifyWorkspace` queried `User` (a tenant-scoped, RLS-protected table) via `TenantPrismaService.root` — the *non-RLS* client meant only for platform-root tables like `Tenant`/`Blueprint`. With no `app.tenant_id` session variable set, RLS silently matched **zero rows always**, regardless of whether the user genuinely existed — so even a correct workspace/email pairing was rejected. Fixed by switching to `tenantPrisma.run(tenant.id, ...)`, which sets the RLS session context correctly. Caught immediately because the scripted verification asserted the *positive* case (correct pairing succeeds), not just the negative ones — a reminder that testing only rejection paths would have shipped this silently.
- Login page gains a required Workspace ID field; signup shows the generated ID in a one-time callout (mirroring the Team-invite feature's one-time-password pattern) before the existing auto-login. Settings gains a third editable field, `tenant.updateWorkspaceId` (format + uniqueness validated, `409` on collision — verified live).
- **Settings visibility, done as scoped**: `nav.settings` no longer gated at all (same treatment as `nav.dashboard`) — visible to every authenticated tenant member. `WorkspaceSettings.tsx` restructured so "My profile," "Workspace details," and "Branding" render unconditionally (read-only text/swatch for non-Admins, the existing edit forms only for `settings:manage`); "Navigation labels" stays fully Admin-gated as before (no read-only value in exposing the raw override mechanism to someone who can't act on it). Verified live: a Member's compiled `page.settings` has **zero** mutation actions, and calling `tenant.updateWorkspaceId` directly as Member still `403`s regardless of UI pruning.
- **Member-experience audit, done as its own real walkthrough, not a rubber stamp**: scripted checks across Dashboard (KPIs + the new chart all resolve without error), Projects (delete correctly absent, FilterBar config present), Tasks (create/update-status actions correctly present per `role.member`'s grants), Notifications, Team/Org Structure (correctly absent), and Settings (visible, read-only) — plus re-running the "does the real Member's materialized `Role.permissions` match the current blueprint" check established after the earlier Settings bug, this time for `role.member`. **Result: genuinely zero bugs found.** One thing that looked suspicious at first (Member's `projects.list`/`tasks.list` both returning empty despite having a department assigned) was investigated against the real data before being written off — confirmed correct: the one real project has `departmentId: null` (created before department assignment existed) and the one real task is assigned to the Admin, not the Member. Exactly matches the documented, deferred §17 gap and the scope-filtering logic working as designed — not a new bug.
- **8 new unit tests** (`workspace-id.spec.ts` for `slugify`). 74 backend + 1 frontend, all passing.
- Manually wrote the `Tenant.workspaceId` migration SQL (`prisma migrate dev` refused to run non-interactively past its "this could fail on duplicate values" confirmation prompt) and applied it via `prisma migrate deploy`, matching the established non-interactive-environment workaround from earlier migrations this session. Rebuilt/restarted the API twice (once for the RLS fix), reseeded (re-syncing role permissions again as a side effect, though nothing new was added to any role this stage), and did a clean `apps/web/.next` restart given the scale of frontend changes (new login/signup flows, a core context-type change requiring a one-line test-fixture fix, same mechanical pattern as `refetchBootstrap` earlier).

#### Session 2 continued — two user-reported bugs from live testing: Workspace ID edit, and assigned items invisible to Members (complete)
- **User report (verbatim, paraphrased)**: as Admin, editing the Workspace ID in Settings appeared "not editable or not saving"; more importantly, when the Admin created a project and assigned tasks to a Member, those items never showed up in the Member's workspace at all — the assignment workflow should let Members see and work on anything assigned to them.
- **Workspace ID bug — root cause**: not a permission-pruning bug (verified via a fresh throwaway Admin that the compiled manifest correctly includes `tenant.updateWorkspaceId`). The mutation's input validation was an overly strict regex (`/^[a-z0-9]+(-[a-z0-9]+)*$/`) that silently rejected anything a real person would naturally type — a capital letter, a space — surfacing only as a small, easy-to-miss inline error. From the user's side that reads as "the field doesn't work." **Fix**: `tenant.updateWorkspaceId` now runs the input through the same `slugify()` used at signup instead of validating-and-rejecting, so there's no input a user can type that fails; `WorkspaceSettings.tsx` syncs its field to the actual saved (slugified) value and shows it in the success toast, rather than assuming the raw typed text was what got saved.
- **Assigned-items-invisible bug — a real, structural RBAC bug, not a data quirk this time.** `isRowInScope()`, `tasksWhere()`, and `projectsWhere()` all treated "own" (assignee/owner match) and "team"/"department" (department match) as **mutually exclusive, AND-combined** conditions rather than an inclusive floor — so a task or project assigned to someone was invisible to them the moment the row's department didn't also match, including when the department was simply `null` (the actual state of the one real project in the live DB). This is exactly why the earlier Member-experience audit (Identity & Member-Experience Foundation, above) found zero bugs here: at that time no task assigned to the Member existed with a department mismatch to expose the flaw. **Fix**: all three now OR-combine ownership/assignment with department match — "assigned to me" is always sufficient on its own, department match is an additional (not required) way to be in scope. This also resolves the previously-documented §17 gap (Member with no department seeing nothing) as a side effect, since "assigned to me" no longer depends on department at all.
- **7 new/updated unit tests** across `scope-check.spec.ts`, `tasks.data-sources.spec.ts`, `projects.data-sources.spec.ts` covering the ownership-floor behavior directly (owning a row with no department match, owning a row when the actor has no department, department match still working on its own). Full suite: 78 backend + 1 frontend, all green (`pnpm -r run typecheck` and `pnpm -r run test`, both clean).
- **Live-verified end-to-end** via a scripted throwaway Admin+Member pair (never the real Admin's password): sent a messy workspace ID (`"My  Verify Workspace!!"`) through the mutation and confirmed it saved as a clean slug (`"my-verify-workspace"`) with no error; created a project with no department and a task inside it assigned to a fresh Member with no department, and confirmed the Member's `projects.list`/`tasks.list` now correctly return both — the exact scenario that was broken. Throwaway tenant's rows and both Supabase auth users deleted afterward (a first attempt at cleanup silently no-opped on the tenant-scoped tables because it deleted without setting the RLS `app.tenant_id` session var first — caught by an explicit re-check, redone correctly inside a transaction that sets it).
- Rebuilt and restarted the API (no reseed needed — no blueprint/seed.ts content changed this round).

#### User retested, reported the fix was incomplete: no real assignment workflow exists in the UI
- User's report: Members still can't see assigned projects/tasks; more fundamentally, there's no way for an Admin to assign Members to a project at all (only a department association exists), and no way to pick a task's assignee in the UI either. Explicitly asked for a real workflow: Admin creates a project → assigns Members to it → creates tasks under it assigned to those Members → items are immediately visible in the Member's workspace.
- **Investigated before writing any code**: the previous round's backend scope fix was genuinely correct (re-confirmed by re-reading `tasksWhere`/`projectsWhere`/`isRowInScope`), but it was never actually exercised through the real app. `ProjectBoard.tsx`'s create form only ever took a project name — `Project` had no members concept at all, only a single nullable `ownerId`. `TaskList.tsx`'s create form only took a title + project — `task.create`'s `assigneeId` was already accepted server-side but the UI never sent one, so every task created through the browser silently self-assigned to the Admin. The user's Member genuinely had nothing assigned to them; the visibility fix had nothing to prove itself against.
- **This is a real new feature, not a bug fix** — planned via `EnterPlanMode` per the standing "no code without an approved plan for substantial work" agreement. Confirmed via `AskUserQuestion`: member-management should be available everywhere projects are managed, not just the Dashboard — the user chose to rebuild the Projects page (`page.projects`) around the same `ProjectBoard` composite already on the Dashboard, replacing its Stage-12 `FilterBar` + `Table` combo (the kanban status columns already separate projects by status, making the standalone filter largely redundant).

#### Session 2 continued — Project ↔ Member assignment workflow (complete)
- **New `ProjectMember` model** (many-to-many, `project_id` + `user_id`, unique pair, tenant-scoped with the same RLS `tenant_isolation` policy as every other tenant table) — a genuine "Admin assigns one or more Members to a project" mechanism, independent of task assignment. New migration, hand-written SQL + `prisma migrate deploy` (the established non-interactive workaround), `prisma generate` run and verified explicitly (grepped the generated client for `ProjectMember.ts` before moving on — the documented Prisma 7 "migrate doesn't auto-regenerate" gotcha from the HR module, §21).
- **Two new mutations**, `project.addMember`/`project.removeMember` (`{ projectId, userId }`, `requiredPermission: "project:update"` — reuses the existing permission triple, no new one needed), both scope-checked with the same `isRowInScope` guard `project.update`/`project.delete` already use. Idempotent add (re-adding an existing member is a no-op, not an error).
- **`projectsWhere` gained a third OR condition**: `{ members: { some: { userId: ctx.userId } } }`, alongside the existing "has an assigned task" and "department match" — project membership is its own independent floor under team/department scope, same reasoning as the last session's fix. `projects.list` now also returns each project's `members: {id, displayName}[]` (a new `withMembers` helper mirroring the existing `withOwnerNames`), which both new frontend pickers read directly — no extra data source needed.
- **`ProjectBoard.tsx`** gained real member management: each card shows a "👥 N" control (built on the existing `Dropdown` component) opening a checklist of every tenant user (fetched via the already-Admin-gated `users.list`), checking/unchecking calls `project.addMember`/`project.removeMember`. Member names render as badges under the owner line so assignment state is visible without opening the picker. Also carried over a `project.delete` action (previously only on the Table being replaced) so rebuilding `page.projects` around this composite doesn't quietly drop existing delete capability.
- **`TaskList.tsx`** gained a real assignee picker: since `projects.list` already carries `members`, no new fetch was needed — selecting a project populates an `Assignee` dropdown from that project's members, and `task.create` now sends `assigneeId` when one is chosen (falls back to today's self-assign default when a project has no members yet, not a breaking change).
- **`page.projects` rebuilt around `ProjectBoard`** (unfiltered — every status, not just "active") in `seed.ts`, carrying `project.create`/`project.addMember`/`project.removeMember`/`project.delete` actions. The Dashboard's own `ProjectBoard` instance also gained the two new member actions.
- **1 new unit test** in `projects.data-sources.spec.ts` (project membership alone, independent of tasks or department, is sufficient for team/department-scope visibility) plus 2 existing tests updated for the 3-way OR shape. Full suite: 79 backend + 1 frontend, all green.
- **Live-verified end-to-end** via a scripted throwaway Admin + two Members (never the real Admin's password): confirmed a project was invisible to a Member before `project.addMember`, then visible immediately after — with **zero tasks assigned**, proving membership alone (not just the task-assignment path) grants visibility; confirmed a task assigned via `assigneeId` showed up in that Member's `tasks.list`; confirmed a second, uninvolved Member's `project.addMember` call `403`s (no update-scope on someone else's project); confirmed `project.removeMember` correctly revokes the membership row while the project correctly *stays* visible afterward through the still-independent task-assignment path (two separate, both-honored visibility mechanisms, not a bug). Cleaned up the throwaway tenant afterward, this time deleting the `Tenant` row only after every tenant-scoped child row, and setting `app.tenant_id` before any of them (the RLS gotcha from last round's cleanup, now fixed in the reusable script pattern).
- Migration deployed, Prisma client regenerated and verified, API rebuilt and restarted, blueprint reseeded (role permissions re-synced again as a routine side effect).

#### User confirmed the Project ↔ Member assignment workflow works end-to-end, asked to continue with role hierarchy
- User tested the complete workflow live (create project → assign Members via "👥" → create/assign tasks → items appear in the Member's workspace) and confirmed it all works, including permissions/visibility. Asked to continue building, "keeping the same architecture and design principles throughout the rest of the product" — the next explicitly-agreed stage was role hierarchy (extends-chain inheritance), deferred from the Identity & Member-Experience Foundation stage.
- Investigated the current permission-resolution code before designing anything (via a research subagent): confirmed `Role.extendsRoleId` is genuinely dead (never read/written anywhere in `apps/api/src`), `applyGrantDeltas` in `grant-resolution.ts` already implements exact `+`/`-` delta application and is fully unit-tested but has never had a caller, and ARCHITECTURE.md's own pseudocode already assumes chain-following inside the resolver even though the real code doesn't do it.
- User specified a concrete 8-tier org hierarchy (Company Admin, HR Manager, Department Head, Project Manager, Team Lead, Senior Employee, Employee, Intern) as a diagram with lines converging from both HR Manager and Team Lead down into Senior Employee/Employee. Flagged before designing anything: that's not expressible as a literal permission-inheritance chain (a role can only `extend` one parent, matching `extendsRoleId`'s single-FK shape and ARCHITECTURE's own single-parent example) — confirmed via `AskUserQuestion` that the diagram is an org chart (reporting lines, a separate concern from permission inheritance), not a literal multi-parent graph to build a bigger mechanism for.

#### Session 2 continued — Role hierarchy (extends-chain inheritance) (complete)
- **New `resolveBlueprintRoles`** in `grant-resolution.ts` — the one genuinely new piece of logic, and it's mostly wiring: for each blueprint role, if it has no `extends`, its permissions are used verbatim (identical to every role's behavior before this stage); if it has `extends`, its own `permissions` are applied as `+`/`-` deltas on top of its (already-resolved) parent's via the pre-existing, already-tested `applyGrantDeltas`. Detects unknown-`extends`-target and circular-chain errors. Returns roles in dependency order so a caller linking DB rows can do it in one pass. Pure, DB-free, unit-tested (6 new tests: 2-level chain, dependency ordering, the full multi-level IT chain, both error cases, no-`extends` unaffected).
- **No changes needed to `resolveEffectivePermissions`, `collapsePermissions`, or `isRowInScope`** — extends-chain resolution is purely a blueprint/materialization-time concern, consistent with this project's established "roles are materialized, not live-derived" principle. By the time a `Role` row exists, `permissions` is already fully flattened, exactly as before.
- **New shared `materializeBlueprintRoles`** (`apps/api/src/auth/materialize-roles.ts`) — ensures a tenant's `Role` rows exactly match a blueprint's role definitions, creating any missing ones and refreshing `label`/`permissions`/`extendsRoleId` on existing ones. Used by both `AuthService.signup` (a brand-new tenant — every role gets created) and `seed.ts`'s existing-tenant re-sync (previously a flat `updateMany` that could only ever *update* roles a tenant already had — it had no way to create a role a tenant had never seen before, which is exactly what this stage needed for the first time: 6 brand-new roles for tenants that only ever had 2).
- **8-role IT blueprint fixture**: `role.admin`/`role.member`'s `id`s are unchanged (only `label` changed, plus `role.member`'s internal shape restructured into `extends: "role.intern"` with deltas whose flattened union is identical to its old flat list) — zero data migration, existing `RoleAssignment`s keep pointing at the same rows. 6 new roles added: Intern (base) → Employee → Senior Employee → Team Lead → Project Manager → Department Head, plus HR Manager as a separate branch extending Employee. `role.admin` (now labeled "Company Admin") stays flat and unchanged — it already had more than the union of everything below it, nothing to gain by making it formally extend anything.
- **One judgment call flagged rather than silently decided**: `department:manage:tenant` has no department-*scoped* variant (the HR module never built one — it's a single flat tenant-wide triple), so "Department Head" does not get department-management rights even over their own department in this pass, only HR Manager/Company Admin do. Giving it to Department Head would let them edit every department tenant-wide, broader than the title implies. Flagged as a real, separate follow-up if wanted, not assumed into the table.
- **No frontend changes needed** — confirmed by reading `TeamMembers.tsx` before starting: the invite form's role dropdown is already populated generically from `roles.list` with zero hardcoded role assumptions, so the 8 roles just appear as more options automatically.
- **Live-verified against the real Supabase project** (not just a throwaway signup, since this stage touches every existing tenant): captured a before/after snapshot of every real tenant's materialized roles, reseeded, and confirmed — the real "Magar Limited" (`magar-limited`) and "Magar Limited" (`yash-1`) tenants each ended up with exactly 8 roles (2 relabeled in place, 6 newly created), `role.admin`'s permissions byte-identical to before, `role.member`'s flattened permissions equal as a set to its old flat list, and the standing Member test account's `RoleAssignment` still pointing at the exact same row (same `roleId`) with zero disruption. A fresh throwaway signup materialized all 8 roles with correct `extendsRoleId` chains; inviting a throwaway user as "Team Lead" and calling `GET /me/permissions` confirmed the flattened, collapsed permission set matched the designed chain exactly (including negative checks — no `user:manage`/`department:manage`, no `project:delete:department` leaking in from a sibling branch). 6 new + 79 existing backend tests + 1 frontend, all green.
- **Found and cleaned up 5 leftover throwaway tenants** (`Verify Co...`, `Diag1 Co...`) from earlier in this session that were never cleaned up after a prior verification round — unambiguous test artifacts, deleted along with their Supabase auth users, same as every other throwaway tenant this session.
- **Found a stale fact in this file**: the real "Magar Limited" (`yashchalke1122@gmail.com`'s tenant) Workspace ID was recorded here as `magar-limited-2`, but the live DB shows it's actually `yash-1` — almost certainly changed by the user themselves after the Workspace ID edit feature was fixed two stages ago. Corrected below.
- Manifest-schema package rebuilt (`BlueprintRoleDefSchema` gained `extends`), API rebuilt, reseeded, restarted.

#### User asked for precise, distinct responsibilities per tier — department-scoped management, invite/promote/demote authority, task reassignment
- User confirmed the 8-tier hierarchy works, but wants real distinctions instead of coarse project/task CRUD: `department:manage:own` so Department Heads manage only their own department (not tenant-wide), plus a detailed spec per tier — Company Admin (invite, assign any role, promote/demote, delegate to HR Manager, full control), HR Manager (invite if delegated, manage employees/departments, no settings unless delegated), Department Head (own department only, assign employees to teams/projects within it), Project Manager (create/manage projects, assign to people who are part of the project), Team Lead (distribute/reassign tasks within their team), Senior Employee/Employee/Intern (work on what's assigned to them). Explicit ask: this hierarchy should be the foundation every future module (Projects, Tasks, HR, CRM, Calendar, Meetings, Documents) builds on consistently.
- Confirmed via `AskUserQuestion` before designing: the delegation mechanism itself (Company Admin granting HR Manager specific extra privileges at runtime) is scoped **out** as its own follow-up — it needs a real data model and deserves the same dedicated-plan treatment role hierarchy itself got; everything else is built now. Also confirmed: role-assignment is hierarchy-aware — a role can only assign roles at or below its own tier.
- Investigated ground truth before designing (dedicated research pass): `user:manage` was one grab-bag gating both invite and org-placement; no mutation existed to change an existing user's role; no task-reassignment mutation existed; HR mutations had zero scope-check at all (presence-only); `isRowInScope`'s `"own"` means exactly `ownerId === actor.userId` and has no notion of "the department I belong to" — reusing it as-is for `department:manage:own` would have silently done nothing, since `Department` has no ownerId-shaped field.

#### Session 2 continued — RBAC precision upgrade: distinct responsibilities per tier (complete)
- **Split `user:invite` out of `user:manage`.** Only Company Admin has it by default now; HR Manager doesn't (matches "can invite if granted that privilege by the Company Admin" — reserved for the delegation follow-up, a single narrow permission to eventually grant rather than a bundle).
- **New `role:assign` permission + hierarchy-aware assignability** (`apps/api/src/rbac/role-hierarchy.ts`, `isRoleAssignable`/`isRoleAssignableBy`) — the one genuinely new mechanism this round. Company Admin bypasses entirely ("can assign any role"); everyone else can only assign a role that's their own role or an ancestor of it in their own `extends` chain — walking **up** from the actor's own role, not down from the target, which is the direction that matters: HR Manager's chain is `hr-manager -> employee -> intern`, so HR Manager can assign Employee/Intern but **not** Senior Employee/Team Lead/etc. even though those sound "lower" — they're a different branch, not an ancestor of HR Manager's own role. Live-verified exactly this distinction (HR Manager, test-granted `role:assign` to exercise the logic, could assign Employee but was `403`'d assigning Senior Employee). Only Company Admin gets `role:assign:tenant` this round.
- **New mutation `user.changeRole`** (promote/demote an existing user) — nothing did this before; `user.invite` only ever set a role at brand-new-user creation. Rejects changing your own role (a one-line safeguard against obvious self-escalation, added even though not explicitly requested). `user.invite` also now runs its `roleId` through the same hierarchy check — no practical effect while only Company Admin has `user:invite` (Admin bypasses it), but it's what makes a future delegated `user:invite` grant to HR Manager safe by construction, rather than a gap discovered later.
- **`department:manage:own`** (Department Head) and **`user:manage:own`** — deliberately **not** implemented by extending `isRowInScope` (would have silently redefined its well-tested `"own"` semantics, and `Department` has no ownerId-shaped field for that path to mean anything). Instead, explicit, mutation-local scope checks in `department.create` (rejects `"own"` outright — creating a brand-new department isn't something "own" can mean), `team.create` (own dept only), and `user.assignDepartment` (can only place someone into your own department, not move them elsewhere). Also scoped `users.list` itself the same way — Department Head now sees only their own department's roster, not the whole company (a real gap that would've existed otherwise: the mutation-level scoping alone doesn't limit *visibility*).
- **Chain refinement, caught while re-deriving each tier's exact grants against the new spec**: the original hierarchy over-broadened `task:update` to `:department` scope at Team Lead — inconsistent with "distribute and reassign tasks **within their team**." Moved that grant up to Project Manager (where a department-wide remit actually belongs); Team Lead now correctly stays at `task:update:team` (already inherited from Senior Employee). Project Manager also gained `project:create:department` (broadened from Team Lead's `:team`, matching "create and manage projects" reading department-wide).
- **New mutation `task.reassign`** — no mutation ever changed a task's `assigneeId` after creation before this (`task.updateStatus` only ever touched `status`). Same scope-check `task.updateStatus` already used, factored into a shared `requireTaskInScope` helper to avoid a third copy of the same block.
- **Closed a previously-deferred gap**: `task.create` and the new `task.reassign` now both reject an `assigneeId` that isn't the project's owner or an existing `ProjectMember` — explicitly left soft ("UI-only constraint") when `ProjectMember` was first built, now enforced server-side, directly matching "Project Manager: assign projects and tasks to people who are part of the project."
- **Minimal frontend additions, reusing existing patterns**: `TaskList.tsx` gained a per-row "Reassign" control (same `Dropdown`-based checklist pattern `ProjectBoard`'s member picker established); `TeamMembers.tsx` gained a per-row role-change `<select>` (hidden for your own row, mirroring the mutation's self-change rejection). `users.list`'s shape grew a `roleIds` field to back the picker (labels alone weren't enough to pre-select/submit a specific role).
- **9 new unit tests** for the pure `isRoleAssignable` (Company Admin bypass, self/ancestor assignment allowed, cross-branch assignment correctly rejected even when "lower-sounding," multi-role actors). Full suite: 93 backend + 1 frontend, all green.
- **Live-verified end-to-end** against a throwaway Company Admin + Department Head + Team Lead + HR Manager + two Employees: every scope/hierarchy rule above confirmed working exactly as designed, including the two negative cases that matter most (a role with zero `role:assign` grant gets `403` outright; a role that does have it still can't reach outside its own chain). Confirmed the real "Magar Limited" tenants' existing roles resolved correctly after reseed (Team Lead's chain-refinement fix and Department Head's new grants both landed as designed, Company Admin's own permissions untouched). Cleaned up all throwaway tenants/auth users afterward.
- Migration not needed (no schema change this round — everything is permission-table/mutation-logic). API rebuilt, reseeded, restarted.

#### User reported the invite flow 500ing — "Internal Server Error" from the Team page
- User tried inviting a teammate right after the RBAC precision upgrade shipped; the request failed with a 500 and they couldn't complete the invite. Asked me to check the API logs and identify the root cause, flagging it as likely related to the new `user:invite` split, role-assignment logic, or hierarchy validation.

#### Session 2 continued — fixed a real production bug: concurrent queries against a shared Prisma transaction (complete)
- **Root cause, found in the API log, not guessed at**: the very first error for the user's invite attempt was a bare `Error [ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT]: Cannot remove headers after they are sent to the client` — a *second*, opaque failure after a response had already been sent once, with no underlying cause shown (the true error had already been handled and answered; this was fallout). A later retry with the same email then legitimately failed with "already registered," suggesting a dangling side effect from the first, corrupted attempt.
- **The actual defect**: `isRoleAssignableBy` (new this stage, `apps/api/src/rbac/role-hierarchy.ts`) issued two queries via `Promise.all` against the *same* transactional `tx` — `tx.roleAssignment.findMany(...)` and `tx.role.findMany(...)` concurrently. **Prisma explicitly documents this as unsafe**: an interactive transaction (`$transaction(async tx => ...)`, which is exactly what `TenantPrismaService.run()` wraps every mutation/data-source resolver in) is bound to one reserved connection, and dispatching concurrent queries against it corrupts the transaction's connection-level state — surfacing unpredictably, sometimes well after the offending code, exactly matching the intermittent double-response symptom reported.
- **Swept for the same anti-pattern elsewhere rather than patching only the reported symptom** — found two more, pre-existing instances that predate this session's work and had simply never been triggered/reported: `projects.data-sources.ts`'s `projectsListDataSource` (`Promise.all([withOwnerNames(tx, ...), withMembers(tx, ...)])`, from the Project↔Member stage) and `users.data-sources.ts`'s `usersListDataSource` (`Promise.all([...three tx queries...])`, from the HR module stage). All three fixed to sequential `await`s — the only safe pattern for queries sharing one `tx`. Confirmed via a full-codebase grep that no other resolver does this (two other `Promise.all` call sites, in `compiler.service.ts`, each open their *own* independent `tenantPrisma.run()` transaction rather than sharing one `tx` — safe, unaffected, left as-is).
- **Investigated whether the bug left any orphaned Supabase auth users behind** (a real risk: `user.invite` creates the Supabase account *before* the Postgres transaction, with a manual rollback-on-failure that a corrupted transaction could bypass) — cross-referenced every Supabase auth user against every tenant's real `User` rows (RLS-safe, `app.tenant_id` set per tenant). **Found zero orphans from today's testing** — all 4 existing Supabase auth users predate this bug entirely (oldest from 2026-07-14), meaning `supabaseAdmin.createUser` was never actually reached during the user's failed attempts; the race manifested earlier, in the hierarchy check itself. The "already registered" retry error was very likely the user re-testing with an email that happened to collide with an existing account, not evidence of a leftover dangling account — nothing needed cleanup.
- **Live-verified the fix under the exact conditions that triggered it**: a throwaway Company Admin inviting 5 teammates back-to-back (the same rapid-repeated-invite pattern), all 5 succeeding cleanly with zero `ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT` in the logs; confirmed `users.list`/`roles.list` (the other two fixed call sites) still return correct data. `pnpm -r run typecheck`/`test` stayed green throughout (93 backend + 1 frontend — the fix is a pure reordering, no behavior or test-shape change). API rebuilt and restarted.

#### Session 2 continued — email onboarding flow found mid-session, undocumented, then hardened (complete)
- **Found already substantially built with zero record anywhere in this file or `CONTEXT.md`** — before doing anything else, a routine "what were we last doing" check turned up an entire email-based invite flow (Resend integration, `User.mustChangePassword`, a forced `/change-password` screen, a global 403-triggered redirect) already implemented and passing tests, dated ~40 minutes after this file's own "last updated" stamp. Treated as real, working code to build on, not re-derived from scratch — confirmed via typecheck/full test run (95 backend + 1 frontend green) before touching anything.
- **User asked for a code review before live-testing it** — found and fixed two real issues: (1) `user.invite` returned the plaintext temporary password in its response even when the invite email sent successfully (now omitted whenever `emailSent: true`); (2) re-inviting an already-registered email fell through to an opaque 500 (`SupabaseAdminService.createUser` threw a raw, un-caught `Error`) — now a clean `BadRequestException` distinguishing "already invited to this workspace" (a tenant-scoped pre-check) from "already registered" (Supabase's own `email_exists` code).
- **User then live-tested and reported the email itself wasn't delivering.** Root cause: `EMAIL_FROM` was blank in `.env` (`EMAIL_FROM= ""`), silently swallowed by `EmailService`'s try/catch into a log line the UI never surfaced. Confirmed the API key itself was genuine by sending directly against Resend's API. Set `EMAIL_FROM` to Resend's shared sandbox sender (`onboarding@resend.dev`) per the user's own choice — **delivers only to the email registered to this Resend account**, not arbitrary recipients, until a real domain is verified later.
- **User asked for a personalized, professional sender identity** — first tried company-branded (`"{Company} HR" <...>`), then the user asked instead for the *actual inviter's* name + role (e.g. `"Deep Magar (Company Admin)"`), computed once in `users.mutations.ts` (fetches the inviter's own `displayName` + role label) and reused for both the `From` header and the email body's "invited by" line.
- **User then tried setting `EMAIL_FROM` to their own real Gmail address directly in the file** — tested live against Resend's API and confirmed this is a hard wall, not a config nuance: `403, "The gmail.com domain is not verified"` — no transactional email provider (Resend, SES, SendGrid, Postmark) ever allows sending *as* a domain you don't control, for anyone, ever. Reverted to `onboarding@resend.dev` with the user's confirmation; the personalized display-name logic already carries over unchanged once a real domain is verified later — only `EMAIL_FROM` itself needs to change at that point.
- **Both rollback sites that create a Supabase Auth user before a Postgres write** (`AuthService.signup`, `user.invite`) were swallowing cleanup failures silently (`.catch(() => {})`) — hardened to log a clear error identifying the specific email that would be permanently blocked if cleanup ever fails again (this exact failure mode is what caused the signup bug below).
- Typecheck clean, 95 backend + 1 frontend tests green throughout. Every Resend send was verified directly against the live API (using Resend's own safe test recipient, `delivered@resend.dev`, for format checks) before asking the user to retest in the browser.

#### User reported signup itself was broken — couldn't create a new company at all
- User hit this trying to create a fresh company/workspace to continue testing, blocking further onboarding verification entirely.

#### Session 2 continued — found and fixed a live signup-blocking bug: two orphaned Supabase Auth accounts (complete)
- **Diagnostic approach**: hit the real running API directly (`curl /auth/signup` with a disposable throwaway company) before touching any frontend code — succeeded cleanly (201, tenant/roles/user all created correctly), immediately ruling out backend signup logic itself and pointing at stale/orphaned data instead.
- **Root cause, confirmed by direct `DIRECT_URL` Postgres query (bypassing RLS) cross-referenced against Supabase Auth's own user list**: two Supabase Auth accounts — including the user's own `deepmagar505@gmail.com` — existed with **zero** matching row in `users`, even including soft-deleted rows. Left over from an earlier signup whose Postgres transaction failed after the Supabase Auth user was already created, and whose rollback (`supabaseAdmin.deleteUser`) either didn't run or failed silently (exactly the gap just hardened above). Since Supabase enforces globally-unique emails, every retry with that email was — correctly, if unhelpfully — rejected as "already registered," with no actual company behind it.
- **Fixed**: deleted both orphaned Supabase Auth accounts after confirming zero Postgres trace for either (a real, deliberately-confirmed-before-acting deletion on the live Supabase project, not a reversible local change). Verified the fix by running a second live signup end-to-end, then cleaned up that throwaway tenant + its auth account fully (Postgres rows in dependency order, then the Supabase Auth user) before finishing.
- **A stray, unrelated process-collision note**: attempting to start a second `nest start --watch` instance while an existing one (from earlier in the session) was still running produced a `dist/` directory-lock `EPERM` — harmless, but worth remembering that this project's API dev server, once started, should be reused rather than re-launched.
- Typecheck clean, 95 backend + 1 frontend tests still green. Only the two real tenants (`yash-1`, `magar-limited`) remain in Postgres after cleanup — confirmed via a final direct query.

#### User paused all new-module work to redesign the org/RBAC model from scratch
- Explicit ask: research and design the complete organizational structure of a real IT company (Company Admin/CEO down to Interns) — responsibilities, reporting lines, decisions, permissions, approvals, employee/project/department/team management, task flow, and how communication/meetings/leave/performance-review should follow the hierarchy — as **the** foundation every future module (Projects, Tasks, HR, CRM, Calendar, Meetings, Chat, Documents, Analytics, Notifications, AI Assistant) must respect. Explicitly asked for a document to review and finalize before any implementation, matching this project's existing propose-then-approve discipline, just at a much larger scope than any single feature so far.

#### Session 2 continued — Organizational Hierarchy & Authority Model: full design document drafted, two review rounds (in progress — not yet implemented)
- **Grounded in the real current system before designing anything** — read the live 8-role blueprint fixture, `Department`/`Team`/`Project`/`Task`/`Role`/`RoleAssignment` schemas, so the design builds on what's actually built rather than an abstraction disconnected from it.
- **Four structural forks resolved via `AskUserQuestion` before drafting v1**: a hybrid model (one universal authority ladder, crossed with department-specific job titles/responsibilities — not one flat ladder, not fully separate per-department ladders); an explicit `managerId` reports-to field as the mechanism for approval routing (role/department alone can't answer "who is this specific person's boss"); document scope kept to hierarchy + principles only (no Leave/Performance-Review schemas yet — those stay their own future module's design session); and a cross-department "Executive" tier (CTO/CHRO/COO-style), which — confirmed via a second clarifying round — needs genuine cross-department oversight (a CTO over Engineering **and** QA **and** DevOps at once), not just a renamed top-of-one-department title. That in turn surfaced a real, currently-missing capability: today's scope-check is exact-department-match only and never walks `Department.parentId`, so a new `department-subtree` scope value is required. Also clarified: "Platform Owner" in the user's diagram meant Antigravity's own cross-tenant staff access — a fundamentally separate subsystem from any tenant's org chart, explicitly flagged as a future initiative, not designed as part of this.
- **`ORG_HIERARCHY.md` v1 written** — a new top-level doc (same tier as `CONTEXT.md`/`ARCHITECTURE.md`): the 7-tier universal ladder (Company Admin → Executive → Department Head → Manager → Lead → Senior Practitioner → Practitioner → Intern) with scope/reporting/approval-authority/management-scope per tier; Department Type as a second, orthogonal dimension (job titles + small bonus-permission deltas only, reusing the exact same `+resource:action:scope` mechanism the ladder itself already uses — deliberately not a new per-department Role-row mechanism, to avoid role-count explosion); the `department-subtree` scope concept and its two candidate resolution strategies (recursive query vs. materialized closure table, left as an implementation-time call); the `User.managerId` field and the generic manager-chain approval-routing principle; and an explicit list of what's deliberately *not* designed here (Leave/Performance-Review schemas, CRM/Calendar/Chat/Documents/Analytics data models, Platform Owner).
- **User approved the direction, then asked for six concrete refinements** — real-company depth and startup-to-enterprise scalability; explicit responsibilities/reporting/permissions/approval-authority/management-scope spelled out per role, not just implied by the tier table; the manager-chain established as *the* primary mechanism for every approval-shaped workflow, not just *a* mechanism; department-specific responsibilities that actually read like a real IT company; the named executive groupings realized concretely (CTO → Engineering+QA+DevOps, CHRO/VP People → HR+Recruitment, COO → Operations+Support); and an explicit, repeated compliance rule that every future module must authorize through this same model, never a parallel one.
- **`ORG_HIERARCHY.md` v2 written** — added a full department taxonomy (§4: Engineering, QA, DevOps under CTO; HR, Recruitment under CHRO; Operations, Support under COO; Sales, Marketing under a new VP Sales/CRO grouping; Finance standalone with no Executive, as a deliberate example of the tier being optional) with concrete per-tier responsibilities for each; an explicit Executive-groupings table (§3) naming the exact CTO/CHRO/COO/VP-Sales examples the user gave; a startup-vs-enterprise scaling section (§6) showing the same model at a 12-person and a 500-person company purely by which rows exist, no structural difference; a dedicated "approval authority" column in the tier table, separated from general scope/management-scope (a Lead has real task authority but zero approval authority, since approval authority tracks the manager chain, not the tier); and a compliance rule stated both up front and inline at the manager-relationship section, rather than only implied.
- **Zero code changed for this initiative** — this remains a design document awaiting the user's final sign-off (see `ORG_HIERARCHY.md` §15 for what the eventual implementation plan-mode session covers: `Department.type`, `User.managerId`, the new scope value + its resolution mechanism, an updated blueprint fixture, and a real migration for the two existing real tenants that doesn't disturb their current `RoleAssignment` rows).

#### New standing practice: documentation stays in sync as part of every stage's completion criteria
- **User's explicit, durable instruction, going forward**: `ORG_HIERARCHY.md`, `ARCHITECTURE.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, and `CHANGELOG.md` must all be updated after every new feature, architectural change, or implementation — not an optional cleanup pass, but part of what makes a stage "done." Recorded in `CONTEXT.md`'s working agreement (§2) so it's read at the start of every future session, not just here.

#### User approved ORG_HIERARCHY.md as canonical, asked to proceed with implementation in phases
